人工智能在埃博拉病毒病防控与应对中的应用:公共卫生优先事项(预印本)

Artificial Intelligence for Ebola Disease Preparedness and Response: Public Health Priorities (Preprint)
作者： Simon Pistrosch, Sunil Munthumoduku Krishna Murthy, Björn W Schuller
摘要中文翻译埃博拉病毒病引发反复的人道主义和公共卫生紧急事件,这些事件要求在不确定性中迅速做出决策。病毒控制面临诸多挑战,包括不完整的数据、有限的诊断和抗病毒药物能力、信任缺失以及受限的医疗基础设施。人工智能(AI)可能有助于防控与应对,但其价值取决于可靠的数据、疾病特异性验证以及对本地条件的适应性。本文从五个应用领域探讨人工智能的作用:人兽共患病的监测、适应性疫情管理、公共卫生沟通与教育、临床决策支持以及治疗药物的发现。我们回顾了人工智能及相关计算研究,区分了埃博拉病毒病特异性发现与其他疾病和环境中的机会。现有研究已探讨了人工智能在生态风险图谱绘制、疫情预测、社交媒体情绪分析、临床预后建模以及抗病毒药物和治疗候选药物优先级评估中的应用。然而,许多提出的AI应用在埃博拉病毒病环境中尚未经过验证,且改善临床或公共卫生结果的证据有限。以2026年刚果民主共和国和乌干达的邦迪布古病毒病紧急事件为背景,我们区分了与当前应对相关的研究优先事项与长期优先事项,包括人兽共患病的监测和干预措施的发展。我们还概述了在明确数据治理安排下整合疫情数据的挑战、在不同疫情和医疗环境中评估模型的困难,以及在不同人群群体中评估性能的复杂性。此外,我们讨论了基础设施限制、伦理监督和社区参与作为负责任部署AI的必要条件。我们主张,AI研究应优先考虑明确的公共卫生需求,并展示可衡量的益处。进展将取决于支持人类专业知识、加强现有应对能力并能在受影响社区所面临条件下使用的系统。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
埃博拉病毒病引发反复的人道主义和公共卫生紧急事件,这些事件要求在不确定性中迅速做出决策。本综述重点探讨人工智能(AI)在支持埃博拉病毒病防控与应对中的作用,特别关注疾病特异性应用和挑战。
证据基础与方法
本综述考察了人工智能及相关计算研究,区分了埃博拉病毒病特异性发现与其他疾病和环境中的机会。它确定了人工智能的五个应用领域:人兽共患病的监测、适应性疫情管理、公共卫生沟通与教育、临床决策支持以及治疗药物的发现。综述还强调了在明确数据治理框架下整合疫情数据、在不同疫情和医疗环境中评估模型以及在不同人群群体中评估性能的必要性。
共识与结论
人工智能(AI)可能有助于埃博拉病毒病的防控与应对,但其价值取决于可靠的数据、疾病特异性验证以及对本地条件的适应性。综述认为,AI研究应优先考虑明确的公共卫生需求,并展示可衡量的益处。它强调了支持人类专业知识、加强现有应对能力并能在受影响社区所面临条件下使用的系统的必要性。
争议与缺口
许多提出的AI应用在埃博拉病毒病环境中尚未经过验证,且改善临床或公共卫生结果的证据有限。综述还指出,在明确数据治理安排下整合疫情数据以及在不同疫情和医疗环境中评估模型的挑战。基础设施限制、伦理监督和社区参与被确定为负责任部署AI的必要条件。
科研与实践启示
以2026年刚果民主共和国和乌干达的邦迪布古病毒病紧急事件为背景,综述区分了与当前应对相关的研究优先事项与长期优先事项,包括人兽共患病的监测和干预措施的发展。

Artificial Intelligence for Ebola Disease Preparedness and Response: Public Health Priorities (Preprint)

Authors: Simon Pistrosch, Sunil Munthumoduku Krishna Murthy, Björn W Schuller
Original abstract (English)
UNSTRUCTURED Ebola disease causes recurrent humanitarian and public-health emergencies that demand rapid decisions under uncertainty. Outbreak control is complicated by incomplete surveillance, limited diagnostic and treatment capacity, mistrust, and constrained healthcare infrastructure. Artificial intelligence (AI) could support preparedness and response, but its value depends on reliable data, disease-specific validation, and adaptation to local conditions. This viewpoint examines five application areas: zoonotic surveillance, adaptive outbreak management, public health communication and education, clinical decision support, and therapeutics discovery. We review AI and related computational research, separating Ebola disease–specific findings from opportunities inferred from other diseases and settings. Existing studies have investigated applications in ecological risk mapping, outbreak forecasting, social-media sentiment analysis, clinical prognostic modeling, and drug and vaccine candidate prioritization. However, many proposed applications remain unvalidated in Ebola disease settings, and evidence of improved clinical or public-health outcomes is limited. Using the 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease emergency in the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Uganda as context, we distinguish research priorities relevant to the ongoing response from longer-term priorities in zoonotic surveillance and countermeasure development. We outline the challenges of integrating outbreak data under clear data governance arrangements, evaluating models across outbreaks and healthcare settings, and assessing performance across population groups. We also discuss infrastructure constraints, ethical oversight, and community participation as requirements for responsible deployment. We argue that AI research should prioritize clearly defined public-health needs and demonstrate measurable benefits. Progress will depend on systems that support human expertise, strengthen existing response capabilities, and remain usable under the conditions faced by affected communities.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question

The review focuses on the role of artificial intelligence (AI) in supporting preparedness and response to Ebola disease, with an emphasis on disease-specific applications and challenges.

The review examines AI and related computational research, distinguishing Ebola disease–specific findings from opportunities inferred from other diseases and settings. It identifies five application areas for AI: zoonotic surveillance, adaptive outbreak management, public health communication and education, clinical decision support, and therapeutics discovery. The review also highlights the need for clear data governance, model evaluation across outbreaks and healthcare settings, and performance assessment across population groups.
Consensus and conclusions
Artificial intelligence (AI) could support preparedness and response to Ebola disease, but its value depends on reliable data, disease-specific validation, and adaptation to local conditions. The review argues that AI research should prioritize clearly defined public-health needs and demonstrate measurable benefits. It emphasizes the importance of systems that support human expertise, strengthen existing response capabilities, and remain usable under the conditions faced by affected communities.
Controversies and gaps
Many proposed AI applications remain unvalidated in Ebola disease settings, and evidence of improved clinical or public-health outcomes is limited. The review also notes challenges in integrating outbreak data under clear data governance arrangements and evaluating models across outbreaks and healthcare settings. Infrastructure constraints, ethical oversight, and community participation are identified as requirements for responsible deployment.
Research and practice implications
Using the 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease emergency in the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Uganda as context, the review distinguishes research priorities relevant to the ongoing response from longer-term priorities in zoonotic surveillance and countermeasure development.

野生动物狩猎社区中的丝状病毒反应性抗体表明几内亚森林地区正在发生人畜共患 跨物种溢出

Filovirus reactive antibodies in wildlife hunting communities suggest ongoing zoonotic spillover in forested Guinea
作者： Grace Hood, Joseph Akoi Boré, Tom Tipton, Zézé Onivogui, Koly Sovogui, Kpadè Zézé Zoumanigui, Joseph W. S. Timothy, McKenna D. Roe, Lian-Ying Yan, Simon David Scott
摘要中文翻译森林覆盖的几内亚地区曾发生多次高后果性的病毒人兽共患病事件,包括2013-16年西非埃博拉疫情的首发病例。森林砍伐和野生动物狩猎迅速扩大了人类与动物的接触界面,增加了人兽共患病的发生机会。在这里,我们整合了血清学、空间和问卷数据,以描述几内亚森林地区野生动物狩猎社区中丝状病毒人兽共患病事件及其风险因素。2017年和2023年,对43个村庄的猎人及其密切接触者(n=511和n=648)进行了筛查,使用了基于多重微球的免疫分析试剂盒检测9种丝状病毒,发现1159人中有57人(4.9%)对埃博拉病毒(埃博拉病毒)呈IgG结合反应,另有49人(4.2%)对邦迪布古病毒(BDBV)呈种特异性结合反应,13人(1.1%)对塔伊森林病毒(TAFV)呈种特异性结合反应,4人(0.3%)对雷斯顿病毒(Reston)呈结合反应,8人(0.7%)对蒙拉病毒(MLAV)呈结合反应,8人(0.7%)对洛维乌病毒(LLOV)呈结合反应,1人(0.1%)对邦马利病毒(BOMV)呈结合反应。并行的假病毒中和试验检测到对埃博拉病毒、邦迪布古病毒和塔伊森林病毒的种特异性中和反应,以及对邦马利病毒、洛维乌病毒和蒙拉病毒的可能交叉中和反应,表明存在多种人兽共患病事件。对卫星遥感数据得出的土地覆盖情况进行混合效应回归分析,识别出反映暴露潜在途径的环境风险因素(包括森林碎片化)。综上所述,我们的数据表明在野生动物狩猎社区中存在多种持续发生的人兽共患病事件,并强调了景观格局在驱动病原体人兽共患病中的重要性。在人类与野生动物接触界面扩大的地区,如几内亚森林地区,病毒人兽共患病的风险更高。在这里,作者们展示了来自血清学调查的结果,以描述丝状病毒在野生动物狩猎社区中的暴露情况。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
几内亚森林地区曾发生多起严重后果的 跨物种溢出 病毒事件,其中包括 2013-16 年西非埃博拉疫情的指示病例。在这里,我们整合血清学、空间和问卷数据来描述几内亚森林野生动物狩猎社区中人畜共患丝状病毒 跨物种溢出 事件和风险因素。
证据基础与方法
Hunters and close contacts from 43 villages in 2017 (n = 511) and 2023 (n = 648) were screened using a multiplex microsphere-based immunoassay panel of 9 filoviruses, identifying IgG binding against Ebola virus (EBOV) in 57/1159 individuals (4.9%), as well as species-specific binding against Bundibugyo (BDBV) (49/1159, 4.2%), Taï Forest (TAFV) (13/1159, 1.1%), Reston (4/1159, 0.3%), Měnglà (MLAV) (8/1159, 0.7%), Lloviu (LLOV) (8/1159, 0.7%) and Bombali (BOMV) (1/1159, 0.1%) viruses. Mixed-effects regression analyses of satellite-derived land-cover data identified environmental risk factors (including forest fragmentation) reflecting potential pathways of exposure.
共识与结论
平行假型病毒中和测定确定了 EBOV、BDBV 和 TAFV 的物种特异性中和,以及可能的 BOMV、LLOV 和 MLAV 的交叉中和,表明存在多种人畜共患病毒 跨物种溢出 事件。总之,我们的数据表明野生动物狩猎社区中正在发生多起人畜共患丝状病毒 跨物种溢出 事件,并强调景观配置在驱动病原体 跨物种溢出 方面的重要性。
争议与缺口
在人类与野生动物接触不断扩大的地区,例如森林覆盖的几内亚,病毒 跨物种溢出 的风险会升高。所提供的证据未报告具体限制或证据强度评估。
科研与实践启示
在这里,作者展示了一项血清学调查的结果,以描述野生动物狩猎社区中丝状病毒的暴露情况。

Filovirus reactive antibodies in wildlife hunting communities suggest ongoing zoonotic spillover in forested Guinea

Authors: Grace Hood, Joseph Akoi Boré, Tom Tipton, Zézé Onivogui, Koly Sovogui, Kpadè Zézé Zoumanigui, Joseph W. S. Timothy, McKenna D. Roe, Lian-Ying Yan, Simon David Scott
Original abstract (English)
Forested Guinea has been the site of multiple high-consequence viral spillover events, including the index case of the 2013-16 West African Ebola epidemic. Deforestation and wildlife hunting rapidly expand the human-animal interface, increasing opportunities for transmission. Here we integrate serologic, spatial and questionnaire data to characterize zoonotic filovirus spillover events and risk factors amongst wildlife hunter communities in Forested Guinea. Hunters and close contacts from 43 villages in 2017 (n = 511) and 2023 (n = 648) were screened using a multiplex microsphere-based immunoassay panel of 9 filoviruses, identifying IgG binding against Ebola virus (EBOV) in 57/1159 individuals (4.9%), as well as species-specific binding against Bundibugyo (BDBV) (49/1159, 4.2%), Taï Forest (TAFV) (13/1159, 1.1%), Reston (4/1159, 0.3%), Měnglà (MLAV) (8/1159, 0.7%), Lloviu (LLOV) (8/1159, 0.7%) and Bombali (BOMV) (1/1159, 0.1%) viruses. Parallel pseudotyped virus neutralization assays identified species-specific neutralization of EBOV, BDBV and TAFV, as well as likely cross-neutralization of BOMV, LLOV and MLAV, suggesting multiple zoonotic virus spillover events. Mixed-effects regression analyses of satellite-derived land-cover data identified environmental risk factors (including forest fragmentation) reflecting potential pathways of exposure. Together, our data suggest multiple ongoing zoonotic filovirus spillover events in wildlife hunting communities and highlight the importance of landscape configuration in driving pathogen spillover. Risk of virus spillover is heightened in areas where the human-wildlife interface is expanding, such as Forested Guinea. Here, the authors present results from a serological survey to characterize filovirus exposure in wildlife hunting communities.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Forested Guinea has been the site of multiple high-consequence viral spillover events, including the index case of the 2013-16 West African Ebola epidemic. Here we integrate serologic, spatial and questionnaire data to characterize zoonotic filovirus spillover events and risk factors amongst wildlife hunter communities in Forested Guinea.
Evidence base and method
Hunters and close contacts from 43 villages in 2017 (n = 511) and 2023 (n = 648) were screened using a multiplex microsphere-based immunoassay panel of 9 filoviruses, identifying IgG binding against Ebola virus (EBOV) in 57/1159 individuals (4.9%), as well as species-specific binding against Bundibugyo (BDBV) (49/1159, 4.2%), Taï Forest (TAFV) (13/1159, 1.1%), Reston (4/1159, 0.3%), Měnglà (MLAV) (8/1159, 0.7%), Lloviu (LLOV) (8/1159, 0.7%) and Bombali (BOMV) (1/1159, 0.1%) viruses. Mixed-effects regression analyses of satellite-derived land-cover data identified environmental risk factors (including forest fragmentation) reflecting potential pathways of exposure.
Consensus and conclusions
Parallel pseudotyped virus neutralization assays identified species-specific neutralization of EBOV, BDBV and TAFV, as well as likely cross-neutralization of BOMV, LLOV and MLAV, suggesting multiple zoonotic virus spillover events. Together, our data suggest multiple ongoing zoonotic filovirus spillover events in wildlife hunting communities and highlight the importance of landscape configuration in driving pathogen spillover.
Controversies and gaps
Risk of virus spillover is heightened in areas where the human-wildlife interface is expanding, such as Forested Guinea. The supplied evidence does not report specific limitations or evidence strength assessments.
Research and practice implications
Here, the authors present results from a serological survey to characterize filovirus exposure in wildlife hunting communities.

由本迪布焦病毒引起的埃博拉病 - 刚果民主共和国 - 世界卫生组织 (WHO)

Ebola disease caused by Bundibugyo virus - Democratic Republic of the Congo - World Health Organization (WHO)
新闻简报The World Health Organization (WHO) reported on Ebola disease caused by the Bundibugyo virus in the Democratic Republic of the Congo on 2026-10-09. The outbreak is confirmed as an instance of Ebola disease linked to the Bundibugyo virus, which is a human-pathogenic Ebola virus. The WHO has acknowledged the situation and is monitoring it, but no specific measures or detailed scale of impact have been provided. The outbreak is part of the broader context of Ebola virus disease, and the risk is noted without explicit quantification. The report highlights the need for continued surveillance and response efforts, as the situation remains under investigation.

埃博拉 暴发,刚果民主共和国和地区,情况报告 #14(2026 年 10 月 13 日) - ReliefWeb

Ebola Outbreak, DRC and Region, Situation Report #14 (October 13, 2026) - ReliefWeb
新闻简报本报告详细介绍了刚果民主共和国 (DRC) 于 2026 年 5 月 15 日宣布的正在发生的 埃博拉病毒病 (EVD) 暴发,并确认由 Bundibugyo 菌株引起。截至2026年10月11日,已报告确诊病例9,066例,死亡病例4,381例,暴发蔓延至伊图里、下韦莱、上韦莱、北基伍、南基伍、南乌班吉和乔波等多个省份。2026 年 10 月 5 日,肯尼亚确诊一例输入病例,患者从刚果民主共和国经乌干达抵达内罗毕,并在那里死亡。由于边境管理松懈和人口流动,南苏丹和中非共和国的跨境 传播 风险仍然很高。国际医疗队团队正在刚果民主共和国、南苏丹和乌干达积极应对,提供病例管理、感染预防和控制、筛查和培训。乌干达已正式宣布 暴发 结束,但准备活动仍在继续。应对措施包括采用中心辐射模型来支持 治疗 设施并加强感染控制措施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
情况报告于2026年10月13日发布。暴发于2026年5月15日宣布,截至2026年10月11日,已报告确诊病例9,066例,确诊死亡4,381例。
地点与对象
暴发 以刚果民主共和国(DRC)为中心,特别是伊图里省,并已蔓延至下韦莱省、上韦莱省、北基伍省、南基伍省、南乌班吉省和乔波省。肯尼亚确诊一例输入性病例,南苏丹、中非共和国跨境传播风险依然较高。
事件
刚果民主共和国于 2026 年 5 月 15 日宣布为 埃博拉病毒病 (EVD) 的 暴发,后来证实是由 Bundibugyo 菌株引起。2026年10月5日,肯尼亚确诊一例输入性埃博拉病毒病病例,患者从刚果民主共和国经乌干达抵达内罗毕,并在那里死亡。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 已导致刚果民主共和国出现 9,066 例确诊病例和 4,381 例死亡。疫情蔓延尚未得到控制,多个省份均出现确诊病例。
应对、状态与不确定性
刚果民主共和国、南苏丹和乌干达的国际医疗队团队正在积极响应暴发,提供病例管理、感染预防和控制、筛查和培训。应对措施包括用于病例管理以及与卫生部和其他合作伙伴合作的中心辐射模型。乌干达已正式宣布 暴发 结束,但准备活动仍在继续。
A high priorityPublic-health news

Ebola Outbreak, DRC and Region, Situation Report #14 (October 13, 2026) - ReliefWeb

News Brief
This report details the ongoing Ebola virus disease (EVD) outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), declared on May 15, 2026, and confirmed to be caused by the Bundibugyo strain. As of October 11, 2026, 9,066 confirmed cases and 4,381 confirmed deaths have been reported, with the outbreak spreading to multiple provinces including Ituri, Bas-Uele, Haut-Uele, North Kivu, South Kivu, South Ubangi, and Tshopo. An imported case was confirmed in Kenya on October 5, 2026, involving a patient who traveled from the DRC through Uganda to Nairobi, where he died. The risk of cross-border transmission remains high in South Sudan and the Central African Republic due to porous borders and transient populations. International Medical Corps teams are actively responding in the DRC, South Sudan, and Uganda, providing case management, infection prevention and control, screening, and training. Uganda has officially declared its outbreak over, though preparedness activities continue. The response includes a hub-and-spoke model to support treatment facilities and strengthen infection control measures.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Help Log in ReliefWeb Search Content Search What are you looking for? Search|t Updates Countries Disasters Organizations Topics Jobs Training Informing humanitarians worldwide 24/7 — a service provided by UN OCHA DR Congo + 3 more Ebola Outbreak, DRC and Region, Situation Report #14 (October 13, 2026) Format Situation Report Source IMC Posted 13 Oct 2026 Originally published 13 Oct 2026 Attachments Download Report (PDF | 306.01 KB) FAST FACTS On May 15, the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) declared an outbreak of Ebola virus disease (EVD), later confirmed to be caused by the Bundibugyo strain. As of October 11, 9,066 confirmed cases and 4,381 confirmed deaths had been reported in the DRC. On October 5, a case of imported EVD was confirmed in Kenya. The patient had traveled from the DRC through Uganda to Nairobi, where he died. No EVD cases have been confirmed in South Sudan or the Central African Republic, but risk of cross-border transmission remains high due to transient populations and porous borders with the DRC. In the DRC, the outbreak remains centered in Ituri province, but cases have also been confirmed in Bas-Uele, Haut-Uele, North Kivu, South Kivu, South Ubangi and Tshopo provinces, indicating an uncontrolled spread of the disease. Uganda has officially declared its outbreak to be over, though preparedness activities continue. OUR RESPONSE Across the region, our teams are providing case management, infection prevention and control, screening and triage, risk communication and community engagement, training and preparedness planning. We are helping more than 110 facilities screen, identify and treat EVD. Our supported sites in the DRC have conducted more than 1 million EVD screenings and provided treatment to 2,806 patients, including 828 confirmed cases. We have so far trained 6,451 people in the DRC on EVD case management, response and transmission prevention. With the support of the US Department of State, DG ECHO and other donors, International Medical Corps teams in the DRC, South Sudan and Uganda are actively responding to the outbreak and collaborating closely with ministries of health, key actors and response partners. International Medical Corps has used a hub-and-spoke model since the outbreak was declared to provide case-management support at designated treatment and transit facilities, strengthen IPC in referring health facilities, conduct screening and triage, support risk communication and community engagement (RCCE) in surrounding communities and train health workers on EVD response protocols. The response includes a hub-and-spoke model for case management and collaboration with ministries of health and other partners. 2026 埃博拉病毒 暴发:事实、常见问题解答以及如何提供帮助 - 世界宣明会

2026 Ebola virus outbreak: Facts, FAQs, and how to help - World Vision
新闻简报2026年,刚果民主共和国(刚果民主共和国)伊图里省于5月15日宣布出现埃博拉病毒暴发,涉及新发现的本迪布焦病毒变种。截至10月6日,暴发已成为全球第二大埃博拉病毒,确诊病例8,625例,死亡4,151例。刚果民主共和国 暴发 发生在面临不安全、流离失所和医疗服务有限的社区,使遏制工作变得更加复杂。10月6日,肯尼亚确认首例输入性本迪布焦病毒病病例,与刚果民主共和国暴发有关,导致患者在住院隔离后死亡。肯尼亚卫生部正在追踪接触者,但表示目前没有 暴发。世界卫生组织(WHO)将这一情况列为国际关注的突发公共卫生事件。世界宣明会正在与卫生当局合作,支持预防、备灾和弱势社区。美国疾病控制与预防中心 (CDC) 指出,埃博拉病毒传播到美国的可能性非常低。暴发 强调需要及早发现、准确的信息和有效的应对措施来限制其传播。
查看新闻五要素
时间
刚果民主共和国(DRC)于2026年5月15日宣布为2026年埃博拉暴发,到2026年10月6日,局势升级。2026年10月6日,肯尼亚确诊首例输入性病例,患者住院隔离后死亡。
地点与对象
暴发 主要发生在刚果民主共和国 (DRC),特别是伊图里省,影响布尼亚、蒙瓦卢和鲁万帕拉的社区。它还蔓延到刚果民主共和国的其他省份以及坎帕拉、乌干达和肯尼亚。受影响的人口包括数千名流离失所的儿童和弱势社区。
事件
2026 年 5 月 15 日,刚果民主共和国伊图里省宣布出现埃博拉病毒 暴发,涉及新发现的本迪布焦病毒变种。肯尼亚于 2026 年 10 月 6 日确认首例输入性本迪布焦病毒病病例,该病例与正在进行的刚果民主共和国 暴发 病毒有关。该患者在住院隔离后死亡。

截至2026年10月6日,刚果民主共和国已报告确诊病例8,625例,确诊死亡4,151例。暴发 是有记录以来世界第二大的。由于缺乏针对该变种的 疫苗,数千名流离失所的儿童面临风险。肯尼亚卫生部正在追踪和监测可能接触过的人,但强调肯尼亚目前没有暴发。
应对、状态与不确定性
世界卫生组织(WHO)继续将2026年暴发列为国际关注的突发公共卫生事件。肯尼亚卫生部正在开展接触者追踪和监测。世界宣明会正在与卫生当局合作,促进预防、加强准备并支持弱势儿童和家庭。疾病预防控制中心表示,埃博拉病毒传播到美国的可能性非常低。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

2026 Ebola virus outbreak: Facts, FAQs, and how to help - World Vision

News Brief
In 2026, an Ebola outbreak involving a newly identified variant of the Bundibugyo virus was declared in Ituri Province, Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), on May 15. By October 6, the outbreak had become the world’s second-largest, with 8,625 confirmed cases and 4,151 deaths. The DRC outbreak is occurring in communities facing insecurity, displacement, and limited healthcare access, complicating containment efforts. On October 6, Kenya confirmed its first imported case of Bundibugyo virus disease, linked to the DRC outbreak, which resulted in the patient’s death after hospitalization and isolation. Kenya’s Ministry of Health is tracing contacts but has stated there is no current outbreak. The World Health Organization (WHO) classifies the situation as a public health emergency of international concern. World Vision is collaborating with health authorities to support prevention, preparedness, and vulnerable communities. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) notes the likelihood of Ebola spreading to the United States is very low. The outbreak highlights the need for early detection, accurate information, and effective response measures to limit its spread.

(© 2026 World Vision/photo by Christelle Lasha) EmailFacebookPinterestTwitterShare Ebola remains a serious global health threat, with an outbreak of Bundibugyo virus disease, a strain of Ebola, continuing in Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) and a case now confirmed in Kenya. In May 2026, DRC declared an Ebola outbreak in Ituri province involving a newly identified variant that had not matched previously known strains. With no vaccine currently available for this variant, thousands of displaced children are at risk. The World Health Organization (WHO) continues to classify the 2026 Ebola outbreak as a public health emergency of international concern. On Oct. 6, 2026, Kenya confirmed its first imported case of Bundibugyo virus disease. The patient, who had traveled from DRC through Uganda to Nairobi, Kenya, died after being hospitalized and isolated. Kenya’s Ministry of Health is tracing and monitoring people who may have been exposed and has emphasized that Kenya does not currently have an Ebola outbreak. The developments in DRC and Kenya highlight the importance of early detection, contact tracing, infection prevention and accurate information in limiting the spread of Ebola. The outbreak is unfolding in communities already facing insecurity, displacement, food insecurity and limited access to health care, conditions that can make it harder to identify cases quickly and provide timely care. For children and families living in affected communities, Ebola can bring additional fear, disruption, and loss. World Vision is working with health authorities and communities to promote prevention, strengthen preparedness, support vulnerable children and families, and help counter misinformation. Ebola virus disease: Facts, FAQs, and how to help Fast facts: Ebola What is Ebola? What is happening with the Ebola outbreak in DRC? Has Ebola been reported in Kenya? What are the symptoms of Ebola? Is Ebola treatable? How does World Vision respond to Ebola outbreaks? What was World Vision’s response to the Ebola outbreaks in 2014? How can I help Ebola survivors and people affected by viruses? Fast facts: Ebola virus disease Ebolavirus is highly contagious and can cause Ebola virus disease (EVD), formerly known as Ebola hemorrhagic fever. The disease is initially transmitted to humans from wild animals. Subsequently, it spreads by human-to-human transmission through direct contact with the blood, secretions, organs, or other bodily fluids of infected people. It can also be transmitted through contact with surfaces contaminated with these fluids. In 1976, public health officials identified ebolaviruses during two successive outbreaks of lethal hemorrhagic fever in distinct regions of Central Africa. The initial outbreak occurred in a village near the Ebola River in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), giving the virus its name. The second outbreak occurred an estimated 500 miles away in what is now South Sudan. Ebola outbreaks primarily occur in Central and West Africa, with occasional cases in other regions. Recent outbreaks have been confirmed in DRC (2018–2020), Uganda (2022–2023), and DRC’s Kasai and Ituri provinces (2026). In October 2026, Kenya confirmed its first imported case of an Ebola strain, Bundibugyo virus disease, linked to the ongoing outbreak in DRC. CDC officials say the likelihood of Ebola spreading to the United States is very low. BACK TO QUESTIONS You can deliver hope and help amid emergencies. Give Now What is Ebola? Ebola virus disease, formerly known as Ebola hemorrhagic fever, is a rare and often deadly disease in humans caused by infection with one of four ebolavirus strains: Zaire, Sudan, Bundibugyo, or Taï Forest. BACK TO QUESTIONS To date, Ebola disease cases linked to the outbreak in Democratic Republic of the Congo have been confirmed in Bas-Uele, Haut-Uele, Ituri, North Kivu, South Kivu, South Ubangi and Tshopo provinces. Cases linked to the DRC outbreak have also been reported in Kampala, Uganda, and one case has been reported in Kenya. (Graphic courtesy of the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention) What is happening with the Ebola outbreak in DRC? On May 15, 2026, DRC officials declared a new Ebola outbreak in the health zones of Bunia, Mongwalu, and Rwampara in Ituri Province. Since then, the virus has spread rapidly, becoming the world’s second-largest Ebola epidemic on record, according to government data, as transmission continues to outpace efforts to contain it. The outbreak is especially concerning because it is occurring amid insecurity, population displacement, high population movement, and limited access to health services in some affected communities. These conditions can make it harder for health workers and humanitarian organizations to identify cases quickly, trace contacts, and provide timely care. As of Oct. 4, 2026: 8,625 confirmed cases have been reported, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. 4,151 confirmed deaths have been recorded. The outbreak was affecting 62 health zones across seven provinces. Ituri remained the main center of transmission, although cases have continued to increase in other provinces. The situation remains fluid, and case numbers can change as authorities identify, test, and report additional cases. The WHO continues to classify the outbreak as a public health emergency of international concern. World Vision is also working alongside health authorities and humanitarian partners to help contain the 17th outbreak and protect vulnerable children and families through hygiene promotion, infection prevention and control, child protection support, community education, and coordination with local leaders. BACK TO QUESTIONS During community activities in North Kivu, World Vision staff reinforced Ebola prevention measures by providing handwashing stations, promoting hand hygiene, and encouraging physical distancing. Community members also received information on recognizing Ebola symptoms and accessing health services, helping reduce the risk of transmission while supporting affected families. (© 2026 World Vision/photo by Christelle Lasha) Has Ebola been reported in Kenya? Yes. On October 6, 2026, Kenya’s Ministry of Health confirmed the country’s first imported case of Bundibugyo virus disease, a strain of Ebola. The patient, a Kenyan citizen who had traveled from DRC through Uganda, arrived in Nairobi on Oct. 3. After developing symptoms, the patient was isolated and received medical care but died Oct. 5. A safe and dignified burial was conducted in accordance with Ebola prevention protocols. Kenya’s Ministry of Health has emphasized that this is an imported case and that Kenya does not currently have an Ebola outbreak. Health authorities are tracing and monitoring people who may have been exposed to prevent further transmission. BACK TO QUESTIONS What are the symptoms of Ebola? Symptoms of Ebola virus disease include high body temperature, headache, abdominal pain, diarrhea, vomiting, and both internal and external bleeding, such as from the gums and stool. It can be challenging to distinguish Ebola from other diseases, such as malaria, typhoid fever, and meningitis. Symptoms typically appear from 2 to 21 days after contracting the disease. People who have contracted the disease can’t transmit it to others until symptoms appear. BACK TO QUESTIONS In response to the 2025 Ebola outbreak, World Vision launched a targeted vaccination campaign to help protect humanitarian staff working in affected areas. In partnership with Congolese health authorities, the World Health Organization, and the Expanded Programme on Immunization, the effort supported WHO-recommended ring vaccination strategies. Plans were designed to help contain the spread of the virus and protect frontline workers and communities at the highest risk. (© 2026 World Vision) BACK TO QUESTIONS Is Ebola treatable? While there is no proven, licensed treatment for Ebola, early symptom management and intravenous fluid hydration can improve survival rates. Ongoing research efforts are exploring potential treatments, including antiviral drugs and immunotherapies. The 2026 Ituri outbreak is especially concerning because the newly identified variant currently has no vaccine available. BACK TO QUESTIONS Health workers support staff during Ebola prevention efforts in the Bulape Zone, where World Vision and World Food Programme are providing vital nutritional support to patients affected by Ebola. (© 2025 World Vision/photo by Jean-Baptiste Mirindi) How does World Vision respond to Ebola outbreaks? World Vision responds swiftly to Ebola outbreaks by educating affected communities, supporting healthcare workers, providing child protection and psychosocial support, ensuring access to clean water and sanitation, and delivering emergency relief. We aim to help communities stay safe and resilient in the face of this deadly virus. A few of our key programming areas include: Community education: We equip communities with information about Ebola prevention, transmission, and the importance of early detection. We help raise awareness to ensure people know how to protect themselves from the virus. World Vision’s Channels of Hope programming: We train and equip religious leaders to share accurate information about Ebola prevention and treatment, dispel myths and misconceptions, and promote safe practices within their congregations and communities. By working with these local faith leaders, who have a great deal of influence because of how much their communities trust them, World Vision seeks to enhance awareness, reduce stigmatization, and encourage early detection and treatment of Ebola cases. The collaborative approach between faith-based organizations, communities, and health agencies has played a vital role in controlling the spread of Ebola and providing support to affected individuals and families. Child protection: We offer psychosocial support to children who may have lost family members or been affected by the outbreak. We work to keep children safe and emotionally supported. Healthcare support: We supply healthcare workers with medical supplies, protective gear, and training so they can safely care for Ebola patients. Medical supplies shipped from the U.S. helped to fight the Ebola outbreak in West Africa in 2014. (© 2014 World Vision/photo by Marilynn Yee) BACK TO QUESTIONS What was World Vision’s response to the Ebola outbreaks in 2014? World Vision swiftly responded to the 2014 Ebola virus outbreak in Sierra Leone. We did this through a comprehensive effort to protect children and their families. With a strong focus on health and safety, awareness and prevention, safe burials, and social and economic recovery, World Vision played a crucial role in combating the epidemic. Our initiatives ranged from providing personal protective equipment and hygiene kits to launching extensive education campaigns and training local leaders on virus prevention. Through our efforts, we successfully prevented Ebola-related deaths among the children and families we supported. And we contributed significantly to the containment of the outbreak. Our approach encompassed immediate medical needs, long-term recovery, and community resilience. When Ebola struck Sierra Leone in 2014, World Vision was already working in 25 program areas. We were serving an estimated 58,000 children and their families. Building on 20 years of community development in Sierra Leone, we joined communities, partner agencies, and every level of government in the battle against Ebola. During the emergency response, World Vision reached 1.6 million people through these and other initiatives: Health and safety World Vision delivered 5.4 million personal protective equipment items. These included suits, gloves, face masks, and goggles to Sierra Leone hospitals and health centers, and gave hygiene kits to schools. Long-time World Vision partner McKesson, one of the largest healthcare service companies in the U.S., donated 200 pallets of medical relief supplies to help meet Sierra Leone’s needs for five months. Awareness and prevention World Vision–trained staff organized massive awareness, prevention, and education campaigns to protect children from the disease through radio and house-to-house information sharing. Staff trained more than 2,000 influential local leaders — including Christian and Muslim clerics, traditional faith healers, and frontline community health workers — to deliver messages on Ebola awareness and prevention. “When so many communities face such terrible suffering, the church must be there to combat fear, stigma, isolation, and hopelessness with both love and tangible support,” said Bruno Col, then–World Vision communications director in West Africa. Safe and dignified burials World Vision and two other aid agencies organized and trained 800 burial teams. These teams carried out 29,201 burials to prevent the spread of the disease while ensuring families had the opportunity to mourn and respecting their cultural beliefs. World Vision and its humanitarian partners, along with burial workers like 53-year-old Ebola survivor Maseray Kamara, were awarded the 2015 Bond International Humanitarian Award. The award recognized the courage and care of those on the front lines of Ebola prevention during the outbreak. “This recognition is a tremendous encouragement after all the suffering we have seen in Sierra Leone and across West Africa,” said Grace Kargbo, a World Vision Sierra Leone burial team manager at the time. “These brave souls have received little recognition at home and abroad. In fact, they have often been shunned, ostracized, vilified because they are burial workers.” BACK TO QUESTIONS To prevent the spread of Ebola during the 2019 outbreak in the DRC, teacher Françoise Mbambu teaches a student how to wash his hands. World Vision distributed handwashing kits and trained the teachers at her school in Beni, DRC. (© 2019 World Vision/photo by Patrick Meinhardt) How is World Vision responding to the current Ebola outbreak? World Vision is supporting Ebola prevention and response efforts in DRC and strengthening preparedness in Kenya, with a focus on protecting children, strengthening communities and sharing trusted health information. In Kenya, World Vision is strengthening its preparedness and participating in government-led Ebola coordination following the country’s first imported case of Bundibugyo Ebola virus disease. World Vision’s staff in Kenya are reinforcing infection-prevention measures, coordinating with health authorities and county governments, monitoring the situation, and helping align public messaging with Ministry of Health guidance. Our Channels of Hope approach and faith-leader networks can help communities access accurate information and reduce fear and stigma. “Ebola is a frightening disease, and it is children who carry the heaviest burden: losing parents and caregivers, missing school and facing stigma in their communities,” said Gershon Mwakazi, interim national director for World Vision in Kenya. “We commend the Ministry of Health for its swift confirmation and transparent communication. World Vision in Kenya stands ready to work alongside the government, county governments, faith leaders, and communities to stop this virus from spreading and to keep children safe. Fear and misinformation spread faster than the virus itself, and countering them is where our community networks can make the greatest difference.” Ebola outbreaks have occurred throughout western and central Africa. (Graphic courtesy of the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention) BACK TO QUESTIONS How can I help Ebola survivors and people affected by viruses? Pray: Join us in praying for people affected by Ebola and other disease outbreaks. Pray for families who have lost loved ones, people who are sick or under quarantine, and the health workers caring for them. Pray also for the organizations working to contain the virus, and for the children and families across the region. Give: Your support can help provide clean water and other essential resources, strengthen community resilience, and equip families to prepare for and respond to emergencies, including disease outbreaks. Time
The 2026 Ebola outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) was declared on May 15, 2026, and by October 6, 2026, the situation had escalated. Kenya confirmed its first imported case on October 6, 2026, and the patient died after hospitalization and isolation.
Location and population
The outbreak is primarily in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), specifically in Ituri Province, affecting communities in Bunia, Mongwalu, and Rwampara. It has also spread to other provinces in DRC and to Kampala, Uganda, and Kenya. The affected population includes thousands of displaced children and vulnerable communities.
Event
An Ebola outbreak involving a newly identified variant of the Bundibugyo virus was declared in Ituri Province, DRC, on May 15, 2026. Kenya confirmed its first imported case of Bundibugyo virus disease on October 6, 2026, which was linked to the ongoing DRC outbreak. The patient died after being hospitalized and isolated.
Scale, impact and risk
As of October 6, 2026, 8,625 confirmed cases and 4,151 confirmed deaths have been reported in the DRC. The outbreak is the world’s second-largest on record. Thousands of displaced children are at risk due to the lack of a vaccine for this variant. Kenya’s Ministry of Health is tracing and monitoring people who may have been exposed, but it has emphasized that Kenya does not currently have an outbreak.
Response, status and uncertainty
The World Health Organization (WHO) continues to classify the 2026 outbreak as a public health emergency of international concern. Kenya’s Ministry of Health is conducting contact tracing and monitoring. World Vision is working with health authorities to promote prevention, strengthen preparedness, and support vulnerable children and families. The CDC states the likelihood of Ebola spreading to the United States is very low.

计算模型警告埃博拉抗体药物可能失去对抗本迪布焦病毒的功效

Computational Models Warn Ebola Antibody Drugs May Lose Power Against Bundibugyo Virus
新闻简报《自然通讯》2026 年发表的一项研究调查了现有埃博拉抗体药物对抗本迪布焦病毒的有效性,该病毒在刚果民主共和国和乌干达造成 暴发。来自多个国家的研究人员分析了 44 个本迪布焦病毒 基因组 序列,其中 12 个来自正在进行的 暴发,以评估病毒糖蛋白中的 突变 如何影响治疗结合。研究结果表明,批准用于 埃博拉病毒病 的抗体 mAb114 (Ebanga) 可能会受到本迪布焦病毒糖蛋白中两个 突变(E112D 和 P116A)的损害,从而可能降低其功效。相比之下,结合了三种抗体的 Inmazeb 鸡尾酒显示出协同结合并保留了治疗潜力。MBP134 鸡尾酒设计用于广泛保护埃博拉病毒物种,被发现能够保持针对本迪布焦病毒的保守靶向。作者警告说,这些结果只是预测,并强调 基因组监测 和实验验证在 暴发 期间指导 治疗 协议的重要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究是在本迪布焦病毒病 2026 暴发 期间进行的,发布日期为 2026 年 10 月 10 日星期六。计算分析基于 44 个本迪布焦病毒 基因组 序列,其中 12 个来自正在进行的 2026 暴发。
地点与对象
本迪布焦病毒病 暴发 正在刚果民主共和国和乌干达发生。这项研究涉及来自乌干达、刚果民主共和国、比利时、法国、美国和其他国家的研究人员。
事件
研究人员进行了计算评估,以评估现有埃博拉抗体药物对抗本迪布焦病毒的有效性。他们发现,一种抗体混合物 mAb114 (Ebanga) 可能会受到 Bundibugyo 病毒糖蛋白中的 突变 的损害,而 Inmazeb 混合物则显示出协同结合和潜在功效。研究发现 MBP134 混合物保留了针对本迪布焦病毒糖蛋白的保守靶向性。
规模、影响与风险
该研究涉及 44 个本迪布焦病毒 基因组 序列,其中 12 个来自 2026 暴发。研究结果表明 mAb114 可能会失去功效,这可能会影响受影响地区的 治疗 方案。Inmazeb 通过协同结合显示出潜在功效,而 MBP134 预计将保留其靶向性并提供广泛的保护。
应对、状态与不确定性
作者强调,他们的结论只是预测,未经证实的临床事实。该研究使用了计算模型和结构预测,结果强调需要 基因组监测 和进一步的实验验证来确认对本迪布焦病毒的治疗效果。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Computational Models Warn Ebola Antibody Drugs May Lose Power Against Bundibugyo Virus

News Brief
A 2026 study published in Nature Communications investigated the effectiveness of existing Ebola antibody drugs against the Bundibugyo virus, which is causing an outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Uganda. Researchers from multiple countries analyzed 44 Bundibugyo virus genome sequences, including 12 from the ongoing outbreak, to assess how mutations in the viral glycoprotein might affect therapeutic binding. The findings indicated that mAb114 (Ebanga), an antibody approved for Ebola virus disease, may be compromised by two mutations (E112D and P116A) in the Bundibugyo virus glycoprotein, potentially reducing its efficacy. In contrast, the Inmazeb cocktail, which combines three antibodies, showed synergistic binding and retained therapeutic potential. The MBP134 cocktail, designed for broad protection across ebolavirus species, was found to maintain conserved targeting against the Bundibugyo virus. The authors caution that these results are predictions and stress the importance of genomic surveillance and experimental validation to guide treatment protocols during the outbreak.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, October 10, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Computational Models Warn Ebola Antibody Drugs May Lose Power Against Bundibugyo Virus by October 10, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 5 mins read 0 Computational Models Warn Ebola Antibody Drugs May Lose Power Against Bundibugyo Virus Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram As a new outbreak of Bundibugyo virus disease unfolds across the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Uganda in 2026, an urgent question has moved from the laboratory bench to the treatment centre: will the monoclonal antibody therapies developed and validated against Ebola virus actually work against this distinct ebolavirus species? A team of researchers from Uganda, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Belgium, France, the United States and beyond has now provided the first systematic computational assessment of that question, and their findings are a mixture of reassurance and caution. Writing in Nature Communications, the group reports that while one leading antibody cocktail appears to retain its targeting of the Bundibugyo virus glycoprotein, another widely deployed therapeutic may be compromised by mutations at its binding site, underscoring how quickly genomic surveillance must be coupled to therapeutic decision-making during an active outbreak. The study, led by Kevin Cissy Nabukeera, Gradi Luakanda-Ndelemo and Syrus Semawule, with joint supervision from Daudi Jjingo, Tony Wawina-Bokalanga, Placide Mbala-Kingebeni and Isaac Ssewanyana, drew on 44 Bundibugyo virus genome sequences, including 12 generated from the ongoing 2026 outbreak. The team’s central analytical target was the viral glycoprotein, the trimeric surface spike that mediates attachment and entry into host cells and that serves as the sole target of the current generation of approved and investigational ebolavirus antibody therapeutics. Because these antibodies were raised and clinically evaluated against Ebola virus, the Zaire ebolavirus species, their cross-reactivity with Bundibugyo virus cannot be assumed, and the researchers set out to map precisely where the two viruses’ glycoproteins agree and diverge at the amino acid level. The analytical strategy combined genomic comparison with structural modelling. By aligning the Bundibugyo virus glycoprotein sequences against the Ebola virus reference and projecting the resulting substitutions onto three-dimensional models of the glycoprotein trimer, the team could identify which residues fall within the epitopes, the antibody-binding footprints, of the major therapeutic candidates. Where substitutions occurred within or adjacent to these footprints, the researchers used structural prediction tools, including resources from Google DeepMind’s AlphaFold suite, to model how the altered residues would affect the geometry, charge complementarity and binding affinity of the antibody-antigen interface. This approach allowed the team to generate predictions of therapeutic efficacy rapidly, without waiting for the slow process of isolating and testing live virus in high-containment laboratories. The most concerning finding concerns mAb114, marketed as Ebanga, a monoclonal antibody that received regulatory approval for the treatment of Ebola virus disease following its demonstration of survival benefit in the PALM trial conducted during the 2018-2020 outbreak in the Democratic Republic of the Congo. The computational analysis revealed that two mutations in the Bundibugyo virus glycoprotein, E112D and P116A, fall within the mAb114 epitope. According to the structural models, these substitutions do not merely weaken binding; they cause the antibody to engage in off-target binding and substantially reduce its affinity for the viral surface. In practical terms, the models predict that mAb114’s efficacy against Bundibugyo virus may be compromised, a result that, if confirmed experimentally, would have immediate implications for treatment protocols in the affected region. The picture for the second major therapeutic, the three-antibody cocktail known as Inmazeb, proved more complex and, in some respects, more encouraging. Inmazeb combines atoltivimab, maftivimab and odesivimab, three antibodies that bind distinct sites on the Ebola virus glycoprotein and were also validated in the PALM trial. The structural modelling showed that odesivimab maintains its epitope binding against the Bundibugyo virus glycoprotein, but that atoltivimab and maftivimab individually fail to bind their designated sites on the Bundibugyo virus spike. Crucially, however, when the complete trimeric cocktail was modelled against the Bundibugyo virus glycoprotein, the analysis demonstrated synergistic binding, suggesting that the cooperative action of the three antibodies together may preserve therapeutic activity even where individual components fall short. This combinatorial effect carries a broader lesson for antiviral therapeutics. Antibody cocktails were originally designed with viral escape in mind: targeting multiple non-overlapping epitopes makes it far harder for a virus to mutate its way around the treatment. The Bundibugyo virus analysis adds a second rationale, showing that cooperation between antibodies can compensate for the loss of individual binding interactions when a therapeutic is deployed against a related but antigenically divergent virus species. The authors emphasize that the complex binding behaviour of the Inmazeb components points to the importance of antibody combinatorial effects for treatment efficacy, a consideration that should inform both the design of future pan-ebolavirus therapeutics and the interpretation of cross-species efficacy predictions. By contrast, the analysis of the MBP134 cocktail offers the clearest cause for optimism. MBP134, a two-antibody combination developed to provide broad protection across ebolavirus species, was found to have epitopes that remain conserved in the Bundibugyo virus glycoprotein. The computational models predict that MBP134 retains its conserved targeting and holds promise as a broadly protective therapeutic against the 2026 outbreak strain. Because MBP134 was explicitly engineered to bind conserved regions of the glycoprotein across the Orthoebolavirus genus, its resilience in this analysis is consistent with its design intent, and the finding positions it as a leading candidate for experimental deployment should clinical evaluation proceed. The authors are careful to frame their conclusions as predictions rather than proven clinical facts. Computational structural modelling, however sophisticated, cannot fully capture the dynamics of antibody-antigen interaction in biological systems, and neutralization assays performed with authentic Bundibugyo virus isolates remain the definitive test of therapeutic efficacy. The team explicitly calls for urgent experimental validation through neutralization assays, work that would require high-containment laboratory capacity and access to outbreak-derived viral stocks. The gap between computational prediction and wet-laboratory confirmation is a recurring challenge in outbreak response, and this study illustrates both the value and the limitations of moving fast with in silico evidence while definitive experiments are organized. The collaborative structure of the research reflects the realities of modern outbreak science. The analysis brought together the African Center of Excellence in Bioinformatics and Data-Intensive Sciences at Makerere University, the Institut National de Recherche Biomédicale in Kinshasa, Uganda’s National Health Laboratory and Diagnostic Services, the Uganda Virus Research Institute, and partners including the Institute of Tropical Medicine in Antwerp, Université de Montpellier, the Pandemic Sciences Institute at Oxford, and several public health ministries and agencies across both affected countries. The work was supported in part by Google DeepMind’s AlphaFold tools and resources, a Google.org AIMaR grant, the US National Institutes of Health, the Gates Foundation and the Lacuna Fund, alongside the She Data Science programme supporting Ugandan women in health data science. This blend of African-led genomic capacity, international structural biology expertise and philanthropic and governmental funding represents a template for how future cross-border outbreak analyses can be assembled quickly. As the 2026 Bundibugyo virus disease outbreak continues, the study’s practical message is twofold. First, treatment decisions in the field should not assume that Ebola virus therapeutics transfer automatically to Bundibugyo virus; the E112D and P116A substitutions that undermine mAb114 binding demonstrate that even single-residue changes at an epitope can reshape the therapeutic landscape. Second, genomic surveillance must be treated as a live input to therapeutic strategy, with continuous monitoring of glycoprotein sequences from new cases feeding into updated structural models and, ultimately, into neutralization testing. The rapid genomic and structural assessment published this week provides exactly that kind of early insight, and its predictions, once validated experimentally, could help determine which antibody therapies offer the best chance of saving lives in the current outbreak and in the Bundibugyo virus eruptions that will inevitably follow. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

乌干达启动本迪布焦病毒病疫苗 I期临床研究

Uganda launches phase I clinical study of Bundibugyo virus disease vaccine
新闻简报乌干达启动了一项 I 期临床研究,以评估候选药物 疫苗 针对本迪布焦病毒病(BVD)(埃博拉病毒病 的一种严重形式)的安全性和免疫原性。这项研究由 MRC/UVRI 和 LSHTM 乌干达研究小组领导,在该小组位于马萨卡市的临床研究中心进行,针对健康成年志愿者。这是针对 埃博拉病毒 物种开发 疫苗 的国际努力的一部分,由牛津 疫苗 集团、流行病科学研究所和印度血清研究所合作。印度血清研究所已生产并储存了约 620,000 剂 ChAdOx1 BDBV 疫苗 候选药物,其中 3000 剂研究剂量分配给试验。该研究正在进行中,旨在解决全球 BVD 流行病防备中未满足的重要需求,BVD 于 2007 年在乌干达首次发现,并且仍然是没有获得 疫苗 许可的 埃博拉病毒 物种之一。
查看新闻五要素
时间
据新闻报道,I 期临床研究于 2026 年 10 月 13 日启动。
地点与对象
该研究正在乌干达西南部马萨卡市进行,针对健康的成年志愿者。
事件
据 MRC/UVRI 和 LSHTM 乌干达研究单位报告,乌干达启动了一项 I 期临床研究,以评估候选药物 疫苗 针对本迪布焦病毒病 (BVD) 的安全性和免疫原性,BVD 是 埃博拉病毒病 的一种严重形式。
规模、影响与风险
该研究涉及约 620,000 剂 ChAdOx1 BDBV 疫苗 候选药物,其中 3000 剂研究剂量分配给该试验。所提供的证据中没有报告确诊病例、死亡或暴露情况。
应对、状态与不确定性
这项研究是牛津 疫苗 小组、流行病科学研究所和印度血清研究所合作开发针对 埃博拉病毒 物种的 疫苗 的国际努力的一部分。该研究正在进行中,尚未得出结论。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Uganda launches phase I clinical study of Bundibugyo virus disease vaccine

News Brief
Uganda has launched a phase I clinical study to evaluate the safety and immunogenicity of a candidate vaccine against Bundibugyo virus disease (BVD), a severe form of Ebola virus disease. The study, led by the MRC/UVRI & LSHTM Uganda Research Unit, is conducted at the Unit's clinical research site in Masaka City, targeting healthy adult volunteers. It is part of an international effort to develop vaccines against Ebola virus species, with collaboration from the Oxford Vaccine Group, Pandemic Science Institute, and Serum Institute of India. The Serum Institute of India has manufactured and stockpiled approximately 620,000 doses of the ChAdOx1 BDBV vaccine candidate, with 3000 investigational doses allocated for the trial. The study is ongoing and aims to address an important unmet need in global epidemic preparedness for BVD, which was first identified in Uganda in 2007 and remains one of the Ebola virus species without a licensed vaccine.

BODIES Govt Recognised In-House R&D Units AICTE Boards & Committees List of Indian CROs List of CROs (International) Govt & approved labs of India List of State Drugs Controllers Join Pharma | Login Home > News you can get e-magazine links on WhatsApp. Click here Clinical Trials + Font Resize - Uganda launches phase I clinical study of Bundibugyo virus disease vaccine Our Bureau, Mumbai Tuesday, October 13, 2026, 17:40 Hrs [IST] The Medical Research Council/Uganda Virus Research Institute and London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine (MRC/UVRI & LSHTM Uganda Research Unit) has launched a phase I clinical study evaluating the safety and immunogenicity of a candidate vaccine against Bundibugyo virus disease (BVD), a severe form of Ebola virus disease caused by Bundibugyo virus (BDBV). The study is part of an international effort to develop vaccines against Ebola virus species for which no licensed vaccines are currently available. Conducted in partnership with the Oxford Vaccine Group and Pandemic Science Institute, at the University of Oxford, the study will evaluate the safety of a ChAdOx-based Bundibugyo Ebola vaccine candidate, ChAdOx BDBV, and its ability to stimulate an immune response in healthy adult volunteers. The ChAdOx BDBV vaccine candidate was developed by scientists at the University of Oxford, with production and quality-testing of the viral seed stock that formed the starting material for large-scale vaccine manufacture carried out onsite by the Clinical Biomanufacturing Facility. The Serum Institute of India (SII), based in Pune, India, has collaborated to manufacture and stockpiled approximately 620,000 doses of the ChAdOx1 BDBV vaccine candidate in two weeks for potential future use. SII has supplied 300 vials to UK and additionally ~3000 investigational doses for this phase I trial in Uganda. The Uganda study is led by Professor Eugene Ruzagira, Head of Clinical Research and Trials at the MRC/UVRI&LSHTM Uganda Research Unit, and will be conducted at the Unit's internationally recognized clinical research site in Masaka City, southwestern Uganda. With more than three decades of experience in vaccine research and clinical trials, the site combines state-of-the-art laboratory and clinical infrastructure, and long-standing community partnerships, providing an ideal setting for conducting early-phase clinical studies to the highest scientific, ethical and regulatory standards. CEPI, SII and the University of Oxford are committed to enabling rapid, affordable supply of Bundibugyo virus vaccines to affected countries and to the populations that need them. Bundibugyo virus was first identified during an outbreak in western Uganda in 2007 and remains one of the Ebola virus species for which no licenced vaccine is currently available. Developing an effective vaccine against Bundibugyo virus is therefore an important priority for global epidemic preparedness. The study further strengthens the MRC/UVRI& LSHTM Uganda Research Unit's longstanding contribution to research on emerging infectious diseases and reinforces Uganda's role as a trusted partner in international efforts to develop vaccines and other interventions against epidemic-prone diseases. Dr. Umesh Shaligram, executive director, Serum Institute of India, said:"The initiation of the Phase I clinical study of the ChAdOx1 BDBV vaccine candidate in Uganda is a significant step forward in addressing an important unmet need in Ebola preparedness. The rapid manufacture, stockpiling and supply of investigational doses for clinical evaluation reflect what can be achieved when global collaborators work with urgency and shared purpose. We are pleased to contribute our manufacturing expertise to this collaborative effort and remain committed to supporting the development of innovative vaccines that can help protect communities at risk from emerging infectious diseases worldwide." KMA 发布关于 埃博拉病毒 的公共卫生咨询

KMA issues public health advisory on Ebola virus
新闻简报2026年10月13日,肯尼亚医学会(KMA)发布了关于埃博拉病毒病的公共卫生咨询,敦促肯尼亚人遵守基本的卫生措施,观察症状并尽早就医。该通报是由肯尼亚首例由 Bundibugyo 菌株引起的输入性 埃博拉病毒病 病例引发的,该病例于 10 月 3 日报道,并于 10 月 5 日导致死亡。KMA 强调,虽然公共集会仍然不受限制,但组织者必须实施体温检查和洗手设施等卫生措施。该建议还警告不要触摸或准备因发烧或出血而突发疾病而死亡的人的尸体,而是建议联系卫生当局进行安全埋葬。KMA 重申了卫生部的立场,即只要遵守卫生规程,就没有旅行或聚会限制。该协会敦促公众避免自行用药、报告症状并仅分享经过验证的信息,以防止耻辱并确保有效的公共卫生应对措施。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该通报于2026年10月13日发布,此前肯尼亚于2026年10月3日报告了首例输入性埃博拉病毒病病例,并于2026年10月5日该患者死亡。
地点与对象
该建议适用于所有肯尼亚人,特别强调公共集会、葬礼以及可能通过旅行或与感染者接触而接触到该病毒的个人。
事件
肯尼亚医学会(KMA)发布了关于埃博拉病毒病的公共卫生咨询,敦促肯尼亚人遵守卫生措施,观察症状并尽早就医。该通报是针对肯尼亚首例由 Bundibugyo 菌株引起的输入性 埃博拉病毒病 病例而发布的。
规模、影响与风险
该通报重点介绍了发现一例输入性 埃博拉病毒病 病例,该病例导致死亡。传播 的风险是通过直接接触感染者的血液或体液传播的,该建议强调需要采取卫生措施来减轻这种风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
KMA 重申了卫生部的立场,即只要采取基本的卫生措施,就不会限制旅行、贸易、交通或公共集会。该建议还概述了公共活动和葬礼的具体预防措施,并鼓励公众报告症状并避免自我药疗。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

KMA issues public health advisory on Ebola virus

News Brief
On October 13, 2026, the Kenya Medical Association (KMA) issued a public health advisory on Ebola virus disease, urging Kenyans to observe basic hygiene measures, watch for symptoms, and seek medical attention early. The advisory was prompted by Kenya's first imported case of Ebola virus disease caused by the Bundibugyo strain, which was reported on October 3 and resulted in a death on October 5. The KMA emphasized that while public gatherings remain unrestricted, organizers must implement hygiene measures such as temperature checks and handwashing facilities. The advisory also warned against touching or preparing the body of someone who died from a sudden illness with fever or bleeding, advising instead to contact health authorities for safe burials. The KMA reaffirmed the Ministry of Health's stance that there are no travel or gathering restrictions, provided hygiene protocols are followed. The association urged the public to avoid self-medication, report symptoms, and share only verified information to prevent stigma and ensure effective public health responses.
Original source text (English)
The Kenya Medical Association (KMA) has issued a public health advisory on Ebola, urging Kenyans to observe basic hygiene measures, watch for symptoms and seek medical attention early. The association has also assured the public that gatherings remain unrestricted. The advisory comes days after Kenya reported its first imported case of Ebola virus disease caused by the Bundibugyo strain. The Kenyan citizen, who had lived in the Democratic Republic of Congo for seven years, arrived in Kenya through Uganda on October 3 and died on October 5. In a statement, KMA Secretary General Dr Lyndah Kemunto reaffirmed the Ministry of Health's position that there were currently no restrictions on travel, trade, transport or public gatherings. She said weddings, funerals, religious services, conferences, school activities and sporting events could proceed, provided organisers implemented basic hygiene measures, including temperature checks at entry points. “Ebola does not spread through the air. It spreads through direct contact with the blood or body fluids of a person who is sick with Ebola or has died from it, or with items soiled by those fluids. A person without symptoms does not spread it,” Dr Kemunto said. The association urged Kenyans to follow official Ministry of Health updates, noting that the guidance could change depending on the public health situation. Symptoms of Ebola include sudden fever, headache, muscle pain, weakness, vomiting, diarrhoea and, in some cases, unexplained bleeding. "Because these resemble malaria and typhoid, only a medical assessment and laboratory test can confirm Ebola," Dr Kemunto said. To reduce the risk of infection, the association advised organisers of public events to provide handwashing facilities or sanitiser, conduct temperature checks and designate a person to respond to suspected illnesses and liaise with the nearest health facility. Members of the public were urged to wash their hands frequently, stay at home when unwell, avoid contact with the body fluids of sick people and refrain from sharing personal items such as towels, cups and utensils. KMA also issued precautions for funerals, warning against touching, washing or preparing the body of anyone who dies following a sudden illness accompanied by fever or bleeding. Instead, the public should contact the nearest health facility so authorities can arrange a safe and dignified burial. "If you have travelled from an affected area, have been identified as a contact, or have been near a suspected case, and you develop symptoms, avoid gatherings," Dr Kemunto advised. The association advised people experiencing symptoms to avoid self-medication and urged them to call the Ministry of Health toll-free line at 719 or dial *719# and disclose their travel history to health workers. It also cautioned against stigmatising travellers, health workers and people who had undergone testing, saying fear and discrimination could discourage people from reporting symptoms and increase the risk of transmission. KMA said the rapid detection of Kenya's first imported case and the tracing of contacts demonstrated that the country's public health systems were responding to the threat. The association urged the public to share only verified information as health authorities continue monitoring the situation

The advisory was issued on October 13, 2026, following the reporting of Kenya's first imported case of Ebola virus disease on October 3, 2026, and the death of the patient on October 5, 2026.
Location and population
The advisory applies to all Kenyans, with specific emphasis on public gatherings, funerals, and individuals who may have been exposed to the virus through travel or contact with infected persons.
Event
The Kenya Medical Association (KMA) issued a public health advisory on Ebola virus disease, urging Kenyans to observe hygiene measures, watch for symptoms, and seek medical attention early. This advisory was issued in response to Kenya's first imported case of Ebola virus disease caused by the Bundibugyo strain.
Scale, impact and risk
The advisory highlights the detection of one imported case of Ebola virus disease, which resulted in a death. The risk of transmission is noted as spreading through direct contact with blood or body fluids of an infected person, and the advisory emphasizes the need for hygiene measures to mitigate this risk.
Response, status and uncertainty
The KMA reaffirmed the Ministry of Health's position that there are no restrictions on travel, trade, transport, or public gatherings, provided basic hygiene measures are implemented. The advisory also outlines specific precautions for public events and funerals, and encourages the public to report symptoms and avoid self-medication.

埃博拉病毒:关于针对邦迪布古病毒的药物测试你需要知道什么| 新时代 - 新时代

Ebola: What to know about drugs being tested against Bundibugyo virus| The New Times - The New Times
新闻简报该文章于2026年10月10日发布,讨论了针对邦迪布古病毒病的实验性药物治疗的持续研究。研究人员正在评估诸如瑞德西韦、玛夫替单抗、MBP134和奥贝德西韦等药物,这些药物已被世界卫生组织(WHO)优先考虑用于临床试验。然而,这些药物尚未被批准用于治疗邦迪布古病毒病,其在人类中的有效性仍未得到证实。WHO已选择这些治疗用于涉及确诊病例或疑似病例的试验,但结果仍处于初步阶段。文章还指出,尽管某些药物,如Inmazeb和Ebanga,已被批准用于扎伊尔埃博拉病毒,但它们并未被批准用于邦迪布古病毒病。文章的重点是需要针对该病毒株的有效治疗药物,制药公司如吉利德科学公司和再生元制药公司正在进行相关努力。该信息基于当前研究,尚未确认任何具体结果。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ebola: What to know about drugs being tested against Bundibugyo virus| The New Times - The New Times

News Brief
The article, published on October 10, 2026, discusses the ongoing research into experimental treatments for Bundibugyo virus disease. Researchers are evaluating drugs like remdesivir, maftivimab, MBP134, and obeldesivir, which are being prioritised by the World Health Organization (WHO) for clinical trials. However, none of these drugs have been approved for use against Bundibugyo virus, and their effectiveness in humans remains unconfirmed. The WHO has selected these treatments for trials involving confirmed or probable cases, but the results are preliminary. The article also notes that while some drugs, such as Inmazeb and Ebanga, are approved for Zaire ebolavirus, they are not approved for Bundibugyo virus disease. The focus is on the need for effective therapeutics for this strain, with ongoing efforts by pharmaceutical companies like Gilead Sciences and Regeneron Pharmaceuticals. The information is based on current research and does not confirm any specific outcomes yet.
Original source text (English)
News Ebola: What to know about drugs being tested against Bundibugyo virus Joan Mbabazi Saturday, October 10, 2026 The search for effective treatments for Ebola disease caused by the Bundibugyo virus is continuing, with researchers investigating medicines that could help patients recover from the disease. ALSO READ: WHO trials new Ebola treatments in DR Congo Unlike Zaire ebolavirus, for which two treatments are approved in the United States, Bundibugyo virus has no specifically approved therapeutics. Patients rely on supportive care, including fluid replacement and treatment of complications, as researchers work to establish which experimental medicines can safely and effectively treat the disease. Here are the drugs being studied and their potential uses. Remdesivir This is an antiviral medicine that interferes with a virus’s ability to make copies of its genetic material. It is being evaluated for its potential to treat Bundibugyo virus disease. The World Health Organization (WHO) has prioritised remdesivir for clinical trials involving confirmed cases. Researchers are also assessing whether combining it with monoclonal antibodies could improve its effectiveness. Although remdesivir is approved for certain other viral infections, it is not approved as a treatment for Bundibugyo virus disease. Its effectiveness against this strain in humans has yet to be established. Remdesivir was developed by Gilead Sciences. In July 2026, the company announced a donation of more than 2,000 vials for the WHO-supported PARTNERS trial, as well as a donation for emergency use in Uganda. Maftivimab Maftivimab is a monoclonal antibody, a laboratory-produced protein designed to recognise and bind to a specific target. It targets a protein on the surface of the Ebola virus, potentially preventing the virus from entering human cells. It is one of three antibodies in Inmazeb, a treatment approved in the United States for Ebola disease caused by Zaire ebolavirus. WHO has prioritised maftivimab for clinical evaluation against Bundibugyo virus. However, its effectiveness against this strain has not been established in clinical trials. Maftivimab is one of the antibodies in Inmazeb, developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals. MBP134 This is an experimental treatment made up of two monoclonal antibodies designed to recognise Ebola virus proteins and prevent the virus from infecting human cells. Laboratory and animal studies have shown promising activity against several Ebola virus species, including Bundibugyo virus. However, its effectiveness in humans has yet to be established. WHO has prioritised MBP134 for clinical trials to assess its potential to improve survival among patients with Bundibugyo virus disease. It has not been approved for use against Ebola. MBP134 is being developed by Mapp Biopharmaceutical with support from the US Biomedical Advanced Research and Development Authority (BARDA). The US Administration for Strategic Preparedness and Response has confirmed that investigational doses are being transferred for potential use in the DR Congo and Uganda. Obeldesivir Obeldesivir is an experimental oral antiviral being investigated for post-exposure prophylaxis, which involves giving medicine to people exposed to a virus before they develop symptoms to determine whether it can prevent illness. WHO has selected obeldesivir for clinical evaluation among contacts of confirmed or probable Bundibugyo Ebola cases. The medicine has not been approved for preventing or treating the disease. Obeldesivir was developed by Gilead Sciences. Opaganib Opaganib is an experimental oral medicine that targets processes in human cells that viruses use to enter and multiply, rather than acting directly on the virus. Preclinical studies found that opaganib inhibited Ebola virus activity in human immune cells called macrophages. The studies also found increased survival in one of two animal models and a potentially beneficial effect when the drug was combined with remdesivir. These findings are preliminary and do not establish whether opaganib is effective against Ebola in humans. The drug has not been approved for Ebola treatment. Clinical trial results involving patients with severe COVID-19 have also been reported, but findings from that trial concern a different disease and do not establish that opaganib reduces deaths among Ebola patients. Opaganib is being developed by RedHill Biopharma, which reported the preliminary Ebola findings in June 2026. Inmazeb Inmazeb combines three monoclonal antibodies, namely atoltivimab, maftivimab and odesivimab. Administered intravenously, it binds to proteins on the surface of Zaire ebolavirus, preventing the virus from invading human cells. The US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has approved Inmazeb for adults and children with Ebola disease caused by Zaire ebolavirus. It is not approved for Bundibugyo virus disease, and its effectiveness against this strain has not been established through clinical evidence.The medicine was developed by Regeneron Pharmaceuticals. Ebanga This is a monoclonal antibody treatment administered intravenously. It binds to a protein on the surface of Ebola virus, preventing it from infecting human cells. The US FDA has approved it for adults and children with Ebola disease caused by Zaire ebolavirus. However, it is not approved for Bundibugyo virus disease, and its effectiveness against this strain has not been established. Ebanga was developed by Ridgeback Biotherapeutics under licence from the US National Institute of Allergy and Infectious Diseases (NIAID). Emergent BioSolutions handles its manufacturing, sales and distribution in the United States and Canada, while Ridgeback serves as its global access partner. The treatment is available free of charge in Ebola-affected countries through its access programme. Vaccine trial starts as Bundibugyo virus outbreak intensifies in DRC - BioWorld News
新闻简报随着刚果民主共和国(DRC)的邦迪布古病毒暴发疫情加剧,邻国乌干达已启动一项针对该病毒的I期疫苗试验。该试验标志着应对该病毒传播的重要一步,该病毒属于埃博拉病毒病毒家族。尽管暴发疫情仍在持续,但现有证据并未提供具体的病例数、死亡数或暴露人数。报告强调了开发针对性干预措施的紧迫性,因为邦迪布古病毒已知会导致人类严重疾病。该试验是对暴发疫情加剧的回应,但其具体影响规模仍不清楚。消息来源BioWorld News是一家可靠的媒体机构,信息基于当前的发展情况。暴发的风险等级未明确说明,但存在疫苗试验表明公众健康正面临日益增长的关注。

时间
2026年10月13日,邦迪布古病毒暴发在刚果民主共和国(DRC)加剧,邻国乌干达启动了针对该病毒的I期疫苗试验。

邦迪布古病毒暴发在刚果民主共和国(DRC)持续加剧,但现有证据未报告具体病例数、死亡数或暴露人数。风险等级未明确说明。

Vaccine trial starts as Bundibugyo virus outbreak intensifies in DRC - BioWorld News

News Brief
As the Bundibugyo virus outbreak intensifies in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), a phase I vaccine trial has begun in neighboring Uganda. The trial marks a significant step in addressing the spread of the virus, which is part of the Ebola virus family. While the outbreak is ongoing, the supplied evidence does not provide specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers. The report highlights the urgency of developing targeted interventions, as the Bundibugyo virus is known to cause severe illness in humans. The trial is a response to the intensifying outbreak, but the exact scale of the impact remains unclear. The source, BioWorld News, is a reputable media outlet, and the information is based on current developments. The outbreak's risk level is not explicitly stated, but the presence of a vaccine trial suggests a growing concern for public health.
